La versión v0.2 de Milestone 2 corrige la presentación y permanece como borrador no enviado.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Evolución del trabajo sobre L-Town\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.1. Concurso 2020: diagnóstico, calibración y referencias normales\\r\\n\\r\\nLas propuestas de los 18 equipos ya cubrían un abanico amplio. Tongji combina calibración, STL, K-means/SPC y comparación de residuos con escenarios simulados para localizar múltiples fugas. Under Pressure utiliza un modelo dual: las presiones medidas se imponen mediante reservorios virtuales y los caudales resultantes amplifican desviaciones. IRI reconstruye el estado por interpolación de grafos en el área A y usa modelos hidráulicos en B/C. UNIFE estima patrones de demanda y localiza mediante búsqueda enumerativa del error entre presión observada y simulada. Leakbusters desarrolla regresiones de presión y optimización de localización, antecedente de LILA.\\r\\n\\r\\nTambién aparecen propuestas relevantes para evitar falsas afirmaciones de novedad. **Tsinghua ya usa VAR**, con información histórica y relación multivariada entre presiones, para representar el patrón normal antes de aplicar SPC. No documenta un benchmark de presión real a +15 minutos ni un horizonte preciso comparable. **Artesia ya utiliza ExtraTrees para predecir presión nocturna media por sensor**, con agregados diarios y validación en bloques de días. Su error relativo reportado de 0,0235% no debe presentarse como MAE de +15 min: los agregados y la validación descritos no prueban disponibilidad prospectiva en t. DHI China usa LSTM y LightGBM para **clasificar el área con fuga**, no para pronosticar presión futura. DHI Singapore genera escenarios y usa una DNN multietiqueta para tuberías con fuga; «pre-detection» significa detección antes de reparación, no necesariamente antes del inicio.\\r\\n\\r\\nCIACUA optimiza emisores con algoritmos genéticos; DandW usa calibración y ángulos entre vectores de sensibilidad; InfraSense formula un problema inverso regularizado; Wu_BSY utiliza descomposición y control estadístico; Cubalytics construye una matriz determinista de anomalías; Zhiyun utiliza firmas de presión y similitud difusa; FluIng combina ICA, detección de cambios y PSO. Estos trabajos explican señales observadas y localizan fugas. No aportan una evaluación homogénea del forecast supervisado propuesto.\\r\\n\\r\\nEl artículo oficial de [Vrachimis et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601), p.18, fija la comparación original: Tongji TPR 56,52%, tres FP y €264.873; Under Pressure 65,22%, cuatro FP y €260.562. Las posiciones dependen de objetivos económicos y de detección; no hay un ganador universal de regresión. El artículo señala explícitamente que el benchmark omite problemas operativos como ruido de medición importante, datos descartados, retrasos y pérdidas de comunicación (p.11). **La oportunidad de estudiar disponibilidad y calidad de entradas tiene aquí respaldo directo**, aunque añadir ruido o sensores faltantes no sea una idea nueva en el campo.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.2. 2022: LILA, modelos de grafos y aprendizaje incremental\\r\\n\\r\\n**LILA — Daniel et al.** Aprende relaciones contemporáneas entre presiones y términos de caudal, analiza errores con CUSUM y selecciona señales para localizar con un modelo calibrado y programación lineal/entera. Hace selección manual de períodos de referencia y combinaciones de sensores para separar fugas solapadas. Es accesible desde la regresión del curso, pero su target no es presión a t+15. La versión ampliada considera 19 fugas iniciadas en 2019, excluyendo las cuatro heredadas; Tabla 1 reporta 17 TP, dos FP, FN=0 bajo su criterio y score €307.852. Su resultado espacial hasta 374 m no significa que todas pasen el radio oficial de 300 m. La afirmación de detección rápida de fugas abruptas tiene una excepción: p280 tarda 116 h 10 min. Para una comparación justa deben conservarse el universo de eventos y las tolerancias temporales/espaciales. Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535), PDF p.4,7–9, y [código LILA](https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Garðarsson, Boem y Toni.** Es el antecedente temporal más cercano. Entrena dos ChebNet con el modelo nominal: una reconstruye la presión actual desde sensores escasos y otra pronostica el siguiente paso a partir de historia. Compara ambas, transforma residuos de nodos a enlaces y aplica ventanas/umbrales para detectar fugas. Explora historiales de 1,2,3,6,12 y 24 muestras; el historial elegido T=3 no es horizonte +15 min. El código `dataCleaner` toma inputs hasta i−1 y y en i: **h=1, cinco minutos**. Reporta 19 TP, 138 FP y cuatro FN, score €326.521. Demuestra utilidad diagnóstica, no superioridad frente a persistencia para nuestro target. Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ifacol.2022.07.203), p.2–6, y [repositorio](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection).\\r\\n\\r\\n**SAM-kNN — Jakob et al.** Extiende kNN con memorias de corto/largo plazo y selección/adaptación de ejemplos, evaluado en diez escenarios propios de tres meses del área A modificada: cinco fugas y cinco fallas de sensor. La lectura del artículo y de `EnsembleSystem.fit` aislada deja ambigüedad temporal; `global_preprocessing` la resuelve. Con `time_win_len=4`, X promedia tres filas previas y Y toma la siguiente. Por tanto, también hay **pronóstico a un paso respecto a la última lectura, con adaptación por lotes de 200**. No debe describirse como reconstrucción solamente espacial. Detecta los cinco eventos de fuga de sus escenarios, pero sus FP son alarmas individuales y sus TP/FN se cuentan por evento; no son MAE de presión ni una evaluación original 2019. La adaptación temporal ya tiene antecedente. Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62), p.8–10, y [preprocesamiento](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py).\\r\\n\\r\\n**GSI + dictionary learning — Irofti et al.** Usa interpolación de cabeza hidráulica para crear sensores virtuales y alimentar clasificación por diccionarios; introduce múltiples diccionarios y votación para evitar que errores de interpolación deterioren el resultado. La versión conferencia genera escenarios de 30 nodos en el área A, con entrenamiento cuatro días/test tres días y magnitudes de fuga distintas. Reporta accuracy hasta 96,54% con múltiples diccionarios. No evalúa el SCADA 2019 ni presión futura. La selección de sensores virtuales puede ayudar o perjudicar: incorporar más información estimada no asegura mejora. Véase [versión publicada](https://doi.org/10.1109/CCTA49430.2022.9966160), p.4–6; el preprint se conserva como versión del mismo trabajo.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.3. 2023: supervisión de enlaces y representaciones de imágenes\\r\\n\\r\\n**Liquor-HGNN — Schaller et al.** Clasifica tuberías con fuga para cada snapshot usando un grafo heterogéneo: nodos con/sin presión, demandas completadas con NeuralProphet y relaciones distintas entre tipos de nodos. Usa BCE y selecciona umbral en validación por score económico; 80% de 2018 para train, 20% para validación y 2019 para test. La Tabla 1 del artículo reporta TPR 47,83%, 46 FP y €271.584; el README del commit conservado reporta otro resultado: 11 TP, 51 FP, 12 FN, €282.785,1. No deben fusionarse. La propia discusión advierte que optimizar score puede incrementar FP. El código confirma etiquetas de enlaces en la misma fila de las presiones: no es forecast de presión. Véanse [artículo CEUR](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-3630/LWDA2023-paper40.pdf), p.7–10, y [código](https://github.com/MilanShao/Liquor-HGNN).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rajabi et al., GAN + SSIM.** Entrena pix2pix/conditional DCGAN para transformar imágenes de demanda en imágenes de presión saludable; construye mapas por kriging y usa SSIM global/local para detección/localización. Genera un año de datos nominales, demanda incierta y partición 80/20; prueba escenarios propios y una selección de eventos del benchmark. El MAPE medio reportado para mapas es 1,42% con 780 puntos y 0,67% con 33, y el resumen indica alrededor de 70% de accuracy de detección. No son errores de forecast +15 min: la entrada es la demanda de la condición a representar. La detección exige cinco excedencias consecutivas, que representan persistencia de una alarma, no un horizonte. Véase [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2023.120012), copia manuscrito p.10,14–19. La disponibilidad de WNTR no implica disponibilidad del código GAN completo.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Romero-Ben et al., revisión y perspectiva.** Además de categorizar métodos, compara enfoques model-based y data-driven sobre fugas de 2018. Confirma que el número, tipo y ubicación de sensores y la disponibilidad del modelo cambian la conveniencia del método entre áreas A/B/C. No se puede transferir automáticamente una conclusión de área A a toda L-Town. Véase [revisión](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.arcontrol.2023.03.012), versión publicada p.19–23.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.4. 2024–2026: generalización, sensores, clasificación e interpretabilidad\\r\\n\\r\\n**GATRes — Truong et al.** Reconstruye presiones del snapshot con atención y conexiones residuales, usando simulaciones y enmascaramiento aleatorio de sensores. Evalúa cambios de distribución, posiciones y ruido. Para L-Town, Tabla 10 (p.20) usa una semana simulada con patrones temporales: MAE 0,9501±0,0086 m, frente a 1,5928±0,0050 de mGCN y 3,4505±0,0129 de GraphConvWat. En el test de snapshots generado como train (Tabla 11) mGCN obtiene meno…967 tokens truncated…; contribuciones por sensor e interpolación laplaciana para prelocalizar; regresión para estimar pérdida de agua. Usa SCADA 2018/2019 y simulaciones adicionales. La separación sensor-fuga se ilustra con perturbaciones sintéticas, no etiquetas originales de fallas registradas. La regresión de pérdida de agua empeora en la segunda mitad de 2019, lo que aporta evidencia de cambio de régimen. El repositorio SIAM conservado ejecuta una demostración del área A de 2018; no se debe atribuirle automáticamente todas las experiencias del artículo. **Recalibración, fugas múltiples y clasificación de anomalías ya son líneas desarrolladas.** Véanse [publicación](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eswa.2026.131450), PDF local p.6–8,12–19, y [código](https://github.com/dorofeev2025/SIAM).\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.5. Fuentes complementarias y puntos aún no resueltos\\r\\n\\r\\n**AGN (2025)** propone atención sobre GGNN/GRU y pronóstico de estados usando presión, elevación y tipo de nodo. Modifica L-Town y genera un año a cinco minutos, con split cronológico 70/20/10. Se consultó la fuente editorial, pero hay una cuestión semántica que debe resolverse antes de usar cifras: el título/resumen dicen demanda, mientras la formulación utiliza estados de presión/cabeza y los resultados consultados expresan MAE en metros. En esta revisión se registra como antecedente espaciotemporal relacionado; **no se asegura que sea solo demanda volumétrica ni un benchmark idéntico de nuestro target**. El reintento de texto completo encontró un CAPTCHA; no se completó. El PDF local y la verificación exacta de target/horizontes/código siguen pendientes. [Fuente primaria](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911).\\r\\n\\r\\n**GASTL (2025)** transfiere aprendizaje entre redes para estimación espacial de presión con sensores limitados. Usa variantes generadas, no un test documentado del target SCADA +15; se conserva como consulta editorial sin PDF local validado. [Fuente primaria](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2025.124475).\\r\\n\\r\\n**hDA-RNN/hDS-RNN (Ma et al.)** pronostica presión/caudal con atención espacial y temporal, diferenciación y descomposición estacional ajustada en train, pero en una red real de CZ con muestras de 30 min; el forecast principal de cuatro pasos corresponde a dos horas. Sirve para mostrar que diferencias/delta y atención ya existen, no como resultado de L-Town. **WeLL** usa Wasserstein en Hanoi/Anytown/Neptun. **Centrality-aware ML** estudia MLP/CNN y centralidad en Fossolo/Hanoi/Modena/Net3. Los tres son antecedentes externos; deben excluirse de una tabla de resultados específicamente sobre L-Town.\\r\\n\\r\\nLa colección multipropósito DiTEC incluye escenarios nominales de L-Town y otras nueve redes. Es infraestructura de datos distinta de BattLeDIM 2018–2019. GraphConvWat es un antecedente de reconstrucción empleado por otros artículos, y EPyT-Flow un framework de simulación: tener sus fuentes no constituye evidencia de una contribución original de forecast en nuestro dataset.\\r\\n\\r\\nLas revisiones de [Vittori et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jii.2025.100880) y [estandarización/benchmarking (2026)](https://doi.org/10.3390/w18182267) apoyan distinguir reconstrucción, demanda, diagnóstico y protocolos. La segunda propone explícitamente validación temporal/por escenario, métricas operativas, incertidumbre y reproducibilidad. Por tanto, «evaluación cronológica rigurosa» también tiene antecedente metodológico: es requisito de calidad y puede integrar nuestro aporte, pero no basta como algoritmo nuevo.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Qué se aprendió de los repositorios\\r\\n\\r\\n| Fuente y commit conservado | Evidencia del código | Implicación para nuestro trabajo |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| GSP/ChebNet, `864a99041af6` | `dataCleaner`/splitter generan y[i] desde historia que termina i−1; `predict.py` usa T=3. Similarly, the\\r\\ninitial appearance of Leak 10 at Pipe p193 in Fig. 4(c) is obscured\\r\\nby the presence of both Leaks 7 and 8 at Pipes p331 and p142,\\r\\nrespectively. For this and other similar instances, we analyzed indi-\\r\\nvidual trajectories of MREji [cf. Eq. (13)] to obtain more precise\\r\\nstart times. The complete set of all individual trajectories that were\\r\\nused for the subsequent change point detection is displayed in Fig. 5\\r\\nand listed in Table 2.\\r\\nA special case, represented in Fig. 4(d), arises in DMA C, where\\r\\nunknown irregular demand patterns are the cause of large noise in\\r\\nthe MRE, obscuring individual leaks and making their identifica-\\r\\ntion more difficult. Hence, we chose to display the daily rolling\\r\\nmean instead of the raw MRE signal for better inspection. None-\\r\\ntheless, LILA is able to identify both leaks occurring in DMA C by\\r\\nanalyzing the raw MRE signal.\\r\\nMoreover, Fig. 6 shows two exemplary MRE trajectories that\\r\\nwere analyzed for change point detection (LI-3): on the left the\\r\\nabrupt Leak 1, on the right the incipient Leak 12. For both cases\\r\\nthe signal-to-noise ratio enables a clear visual identification of the\\r\\nleak’s development. Looking at the daily rolling means facilitates\\r\\nthe visual inspection. It is worth noting that in the presence of either\\r\\nexemplary leak, further leaks started in different parts of the same\\r\\nDMA. Yet the trajectories enable making a clear distinction between\\r\\noverlapping leaks and are therefore well suited for the application of\\r\\ndifferent change point detection methods. For a more detailed dis-\\r\\ncussion on the effects of simultaneous leaks, we refer the reader\\r\\nto the “Discussion” section.\\r\\nFig. 7 compares the results of expert and Cusum change\\r\\npoint annotation for abrupt [Figs. 7(a and c)] and incipient\\r\\n[Figs. 7(b and d)] leaks in terms of both TTD [Figs. 7(a and b)]\\r\\nand Vleaked [Figs. 7(c and d)]. The expert\\r\\nlabeling was performed ex-post, in contrast to the online Cusum\\r\\nmethod. This implicit assumption of perfect foresight for the\\r\\nexpert-based annotations makes the approach infeasible in practice.\\r\\nFurthermore, it would be impract…2740 tokens truncated…l information about the piping system. This\\r\\neffectively describes a graph-signal reconstruction problem\\r\\nand can be addressed with the application of GNNs. In\\r\\nthe following section, we first introduce the field of GNNs,\\r\\nmotivating our architectural choices. We then conclude the\\r\\nsection with the proposed methodology.\\r\\n2.2 Graph Signal Reconstruction and Prediction\\r\\nGraph Neural Networks\\r\\nGNNs are neural network archi-\\r\\ntectures that can process geometrical data (non-Euclidean)\\r\\nwith the goal of extracting node features combined with\\r\\ngraph topology to solve tasks such as prediction, forecast-\\r\\ning and classification (Ortega et al. (2018)). In the litera-\\r\\nture, their architectures can be classified into spatial- and\\r\\nspectral-based GNNs. The former, implement a message-\\r\\npassing process along the graph at each graph-convolution\\r\\noperation, which corresponds to the multiplication of a\\r\\nconvolution kernel with the corresponding node feature\\r\\nvectors, followed by a sum or a mean rule. These method-\\r\\nologies are very simple and adapt to different network\\r\\ntopologies, however they suffer from a well known over-\\r\\nsmoothing problem. In the case of deep networks with\\r\\nmany hidden layers, the extracted features assimilate\\r\\nacross the graph. The latter, spectral-based architectures,\\r\\nbuild on multiple hidden layers, each one performing\\r\\nspectral convolutions, defined from a GSP point of view.\\r\\nGSP provides a notion of frequency and a graph Fourier\\r\\ntransform, allowing for filtering operation in the spectral\\r\\n(frequency) domain. As a consequence, a graph convolu-\\r\\ntional layer, can be written by a sum of filtered signals\\r\\nfollowed by an activation function, where each filter is\\r\\ndefined in the graph spectral domain (using an eigen-\\r\\ndecomposition of a graph Laplacian). These networks alle-\\r\\nviate the oversmoothing effect, while suffering from a large\\r\\ncomputational burden induced by the forward/inverse\\r\\ngraph Fourier transform. To simplify the computational\\r\\ncomplexity, some approaches based on parameterisation\\r\\nusing Chebyshev polynomials Defferrard et al. (2017) have\\r\\nbeen proposed, known as the ChebNet model.\\r\\nOur Architecture\\r\\nFor a very sparsely monitored WDN,\\r\\nas the one in our work, a deeper network, consisting of\\r\\nmore hidden layers, might be needed. At the same time,\\r\\nto ensure a localisation of the leakage, we need to preserve\\r\\nas much as local information as possible. Oversmoothing\\r\\nwould not allow us to preserve high-frequency (hence local)\\r\\ninformation, thus we adopt spectral-based architectures.\\r\\nOur initial ChebNet model is based on the one provided\\r\\nby Hajgató et al. (2021) for the WDN of Richmond, which\\r\\nPAGE 4\\r\\n664\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\\r\\nFig. 3. ChebNet model for graph signal reconstruction.\\r\\nThe predictor (Fig. 1) has the same structure but the\\r\\ninput channel chin, has a depth of 3 time periods.\\r\\nhas a similar number of junctions and pipes as the WDN\\r\\nwe benchmark our model on. The model consists solely of\\r\\nChebyshev convolutional layers, which are regularised by\\r\\nthe weight decay of the gradient descent optimiser. Layer\\r\\nweights are initialised by the Xavier normal distribution\\r\\n(Glorot and Bengio (2010)) and biases set to zero. The\\r\\nhidden layers are activated with a SiLU sigmoid linear\\r\\nunit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer\\r\\nis passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-\\r\\nmiser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties\\r\\nof having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that\\r\\nadjusts to the function to be optimised.\\r\\nAn exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-\\r\\nters was provided by Hajgató et al. (2021) leading to the\\r\\nfollowing architecture for the WDN of Richmond: 4 Cheby-\\r\\nshev spectral graph convolutional layers with the degree of\\r\\nthe polynomial in the hidden layers set to [K1, K2, K3, ] =\\r\\n[240, 120, 20], and filter sizes [F1, F2, F3] = [120, 60, 30].\\r\\nThis forms the basis of our architecture, depicted in Fig. 3.\\r\\nThe input channel size, chin, here refers to the depth of\\r\\nthe input; in the case of the reconstructor, this will then\\r\\nequal one, as a single input graph is used to generate the\\r\\noutput, whereas for the predictor, this will be equivalent\\r\\nto the T number of time step used to generate the out-\\r\\nput prediction. From this basis, we optimised the graph\\r\\nconstruction methodologies (weights assignment, signal\\r\\nscaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for\\r\\nobserved nodes) as motivated in Sec. 2.1, and the window\\r\\nsize for the predictor.\\r\\nDetection and Localisation Methodology\\r\\nWe now for-\\r\\nmalise the proposed method with Algorithm 1 and focus\\r\\non i) the estimation error, ii) the conversion from node\\r\\nto edge error (or residual), iii) fault detection and leakage\\r\\nlocalisation.\\r\\nNode Estimation Error Evaluation\\r\\nThe outputs of the\\r\\npredictor and reconstructor are compared, by evaluating\\r\\nat each iteration the difference between the reconstructed\\r\\npressure at time t with the predicted one, which was\\r\\ngenerated at t −1 using past information. The difference\\r\\nis the prediction error or residual signal, namely r(t) =\\r\\n[r1(t), r2(t), . . . , rN(t)] = ˆy(t)\\r\\np\\r\\n−ˆy(t)\\r\\nr . Under nominal non-\\r\\nfaulty conditions, the residual error should have stationary\\r\\nbehavior and should be caused mainly by the presence\\r\\nof random noise. When this residual does not display a\\r\\nstationary behavior anymore, this implies a change in the\\r\\nunderlying model describing the behaviour of the WDN.\\r\\nIn this paper, we identify this change as the presence of a\\r\\nleakage.\\r\\nAlgorithm 1 Leak detection pseudocode\\r\\n1: for every_timestep : t do\\r\\n2:\\r\\nreadings ←readPressureSensors()\\r\\n3:\\r\\nif first_run then\\r\\n4:\\r\\nnext_prediction ←GNN_1.predict(readi…148 tokens truncated…hen\\r\\n19:\\r\\nLeak detected, log edge, v.\\r\\n20:\\r\\nend if\\r\\n21:\\r\\nend for\\r\\n22:\\r\\n// Filter duplicate alarms\\r\\n23:\\r\\nfor faulty_edges : v do\\r\\n24:\\r\\nfor neighbour in k-hop distance of v do\\r\\n25:\\r\\nif neighbour has fault in the past n steps then\\r\\n26:\\r\\nSuppress fault alarms from edge\\r\\n27:\\r\\nend if\\r\\n28:\\r\\nend for\\r\\n29:\\r\\nend for\\r\\n30:\\r\\nnext_prediction ←GNN_1.predict(readings)\\r\\n31:\\r\\nend if\\r\\n32:\\r\\nRaise alarm for edges v that are in alarm state\\r\\n33: end for\\r\\nEdge error evaluation\\r\\nInstead of considering the residual\\r\\nsignal at each node, a novel way to process the residuals\\r\\nis proposed, whereby the signals are considered on a per-\\r\\nedge basis, rather than a node one. This stems from the\\r\\nnotion that leaks commonly happen along a pipe rather\\r\\nthan at a junction, and for it being directly applicable\\r\\nto the benchmark dataset. In the case of an edge euv,\\r\\nconnecting the two neighbouring nodes v and u, we would\\r\\nconsider its residual as r(E)\\r\\nuv (t) as r(E)\\r\\nuv (t) = rv(t) −ru(t).\\r\\nAssess fault condition. Once the edge error is evaluated,\\r\\nwe need to detect possible faulty conditions. The strategy\\r\\nconsists of calculating the moving average of the residual\\r\\nsignal r(E)\\r\\nuv (t) of a given edge uv in the network, for a\\r\\nwindow of size m at observation time t, 1 as per ¯r(E)\\r\\nuv (t) =\\r\\n1/m \\u001fm−1\\r\\nj=0 r(E)\\r\\nuv (t −j) and in defining a detection logic as:\\r\\nFD =\\r\\n\\r\\n0\\r\\nif |¯r(E)\\r\\nuv (t)| < ¯ρuv(t)\\r\\n1\\r\\notherwise\\r\\n(1)\\r\\nwhere, ¯ρuv(t) = α¯σuv(t), and α is a parameter that allows\\r\\nto tune the acceptable false alarm rate based on Chebyshev\\r\\ninequalities, with ¯σ(t)\\r\\nuv being the rolling standard deviation\\r\\nof the residual signal for a window of size m. This test\\r\\nis then individually applied on each residual signal at\\r\\nevery time step to obtain a classification of whether the\\r\\nsignal is deemed indicative of a leak being present in its\\r\\nneighbourhood or not.\\r\\n1 For the sake of notation simplicity, we omit the dependence on m.\\r\\nPAGE 5\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\\r\\n665\\r\\nFig. 4. The measurements are\\r\\nnormalised to the interval [0, 1], but operating pressure in L-Town is normally between ∼20 −70 m.\\r\\n3. SIMULATION RESULTS\\r\\nWe validate our method on the BattLeDIM, Battle of\\r\\nthe Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods, challenge\\r\\n(Vrachimis et al. (2020)). Its dataset comes from the hy-\\r\\npothetical L-Town, with a WDN spanning a total pipe\\r\\nlength of 42, 6 km. The network is segmented into three\\r\\nareas and composed of 782 nodes. The WDN is further\\r\\ninstrumented with, one tank level sensor, three flow sen-\\r\\nsors, and 33 pressure sensors. The competition objective\\r\\nis to detect as many of the leaks that occurred in the 2019\\r\\ndataset as possible. For this, researchers are provided with\\r\\na nominal model of the WDN, contained in an EPANET\\r\\nhydraulic simulation input file, which has ±10% inherent\\r\\nuncertainties, due to demand and topological ambiguity.\\r\\nThis nominal model is used for the training of the GNN\\r\\nreconstructor and predictor, as per Sec. 2.2. Competitors\\r\\nare further given sensor measurements from the WDN’s\\r\\ncontrol system related to the year of 2018, along with\\r\\na documented leakage dataset for 2018, of when a leak\\r\\noccurred in the network, when it was fixed and its es-\\r\\ntimated size. The considered leakages include background\\r\\nleaks measuring 1−5% of the network inflow, medium pipe\\r\\nbursts representing 5 −10% of the average system inflow,\\r\\nand large pipe bursts of entity > 10%. The average inflow\\r\\nof the system is ∼180m3/h. Both abrupt and incipient\\r\\nleakages occur in the dataset. A scoring function is further\\r\\nsupplied in the challenge to evaluate the accuracy of the\\r\\nmethods, converting the scores into Euros saved from\\r\\nnon-revenue water and reparation costs, by the assessed\\r\\ndetection method. The sooner a leak is detected and the\\r\\ncloser it is to the actual source, the higher the economic\\r\\nreward. True positives are defined as detection alarms\\r\\nraised during the lifetime of a leakage and within a distance\\r\\nof 300 m from the leakage location.\\r\\nEquipped with this competition dataset and model, the\\r\\nproposed GNN reconstructor has been trained. For what\\r\\nFig. 5. Residual signal of node 1 for the year 2018.\\r\\nconcerns the predictor GNN, a window of past partially\\r\\nobserved measurements is needed for the inference of nodal\\r\\npressures at the next time step. The size of the window T\\r\\nhas been selected during the training phase, considering\\r\\nvalues of T = [1, 2, 3, 6, 12, 24], corresponding to windows\\r\\nof 5 min up to 2 hr being used as the input to the GNN,\\r\\nsince the data is sampled at 5 min intervals. A value of\\r\\nT = 3 was then selected.\\r\\nThe model is then tested on unseen data from the his-\\r\\ntorical pressure measurements of 2019. Fig. 4 shows one\\r\\ninferred pressure scene for the 782 nodes, from a single\\r\\nobservation of 33 measurements. We then analysed node\\r\\nresidual signals rv(t) at each node v, which are computed\\r\\nat every timestep. An example can be seen for node 1\\r\\nin Fig. 5. The edge-wise residual signals of pipes in the\\r\\nneighbourhood of a leak in pipe 31 is shown in Fig. 6.\\r\\nAs evident from Fig. 6, the signal mean values are not\\r\\nequal to zero. We deduct the mean value computed at\\r\\neach single pipe only from observations when the pipe\\r\\nis known to be in a non-faulty state, and its neighbors\\r\\ntoo. Edge-wise residual analysis is then performed on the\\r\\nmoving averages of the signals for leak detection. The\\r\\nrolling means ¯rv(t) are calculated described in the previous\\r\\nsection and the detection depends on the condition in\\r\\nEq. 1. There are two parameters that need to be set in the\\r\\ndetection method: α the false alarm rate parameter that\\r\\nscales the detection threshold, and the size of the window\\r\\nfor calculating the moving average and threshold.\\r\\nA window size of m = 7 days and a false alarm rate\\r\\nparameter α = 1.0 are chosen. The leakage candidates\\r\\nare filtered, so that no more than a single detection\\r\\nmay be returned on a given day in a neighborhood of\\r\\n6 hops. The reasoning behind this approach is the in-\\r\\ntuition that information of the leakage propagates the\\r\\nnetwork from the leakage origin, thus causing false-alarms\\r\\nin areas non-related to the leakage. The simulation results\\r\\nobtained with this configuration led to an Economic score\\r\\nof e326.521, 19 True positives, 138 False positives, and\\r\\n4 False negatives, as shown in Table 1. It is worth noting\\r\\nthat with respect to the teams in the BattleDIM challenge,\\r\\nour method only uses pressure measurements, while other\\r\\nentries also used flow measurements and smart meter mea-\\r\\nsurements. We recognise that the proposed method suffers\\r\\nfrom false positives, with 138 false alarms given over the\\r\\nyear. We\\r\\nbuild and simulate 10 scenarios where the first 5 scenarios each contain a single\\r\\nleakage – we vary position, time and size of the leakage – and the remaining 5\\r\\nscenarios each contain a single diﬀerent sensor fault (position is varied):\\r\\n– Scenario 6: Sensor measurement is overflowing over time – i.e. it is going to\\r\\ninfinity over time.\\r\\n– Scenario 7: Gaussian noise is added to the sensor measurement.\\r\\n– Scenario 8: A constant value is added to the sensor measurement.\\r\\n– Scenario 9: Sensor measurement is set equal to zero.\\r\\n– Scenario 10: Sensor measurement is shifted by a small amount.\\r\\nEach scenario is simulated (using WNTR [8]) for 3 months and pressure sensors\\r\\nare sampled every 5 min.\\r\\n1 Implementation is available on GitHub: https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-\\r\\nkNN-Regressor OnlineLearning WDNs.\\r\\nPAGE 9\\r\\nSAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks\\r\\n759\\r\\nFig. 1. L-Town network [16] – we only use “Area A” where we have 29 pressure sensors.\\r\\nThe data stream of sensor measurements is post processed by using a sliding\\r\\nwindow of size 4 – we average all samples dimensional wise in this time window,\\r\\nso that we end up with 29 dimensional samples.\\r\\n5.2\\r\\nSetup\\r\\nWe compare our proposed SAM-kNN regressor (see Sect. 4) to several other\\r\\nonline learning regressors. In order to justify the introduced overhead of the SAM\\r\\narchitecture, we compare its performance to vanilla regressors (kNN regression\\r\\nand linear regression) wrapped as online learners by using the river toolbox2.\\r\\nFor each pressure sensor, we build a corresponding virtual sensor based on\\r\\nall other pressure sensors – i.e. we try to predict (using a regressor) the pressure\\r\\nbased on the past pressure values of all other pressure sensors (see Sect. 3).\\r\\nThese virtual sensors are then used for a residual based anomaly detection –\\r\\ni.e. an alarm (detected anomaly) is raised when the predicted pressure value\\r\\ndeviates too much from the observed pressure measurement. For each scenario,\\r\\nthe processed data stream is fed as batches of 200 samples to the regressors\\r\\n(realizing the virtual sensors).\\r\\nFor each regressor, we evaluate the performance of the resulting anomaly\\r\\ndetector – since we are interested in the detection of a single anomaly (leakage\\r\\nand sensor fault), we report true positives (TP), false positives (FP) and false\\r\\nnegatives (FN). Note that the true positives and false negatives are always either\\r\\n0 or 1 because we only check whether an alarm was raised when the single\\r\\nanomaly was present or not – however, for the false positives, we count every\\r\\nsingle false alarm.\\r\\n2 https://github.com/online-ml/river.\\r\\nPAGE 10\\r\\n760\\r\\nJ. Jakob et al.\\r\\n5.3\\r\\nResults\\r\\nThe results for leakage detection, for each regressor and each scenario, are shown\\r\\nin Table 1. Likewise, the results for sensor fault detection are shown in Table 2.\\r\\nWe observe the same/similar eﬀects for both types of anomalies (leakages and\\r\\nsensor faults): We observe that linear regression completely fails to detect the\\r\\nanomaly – this indicates that a linear model is not suﬃcient to model the\\r\\nhydraulic dynamics in the water distribution network and hence fails to detect\\r\\nany anomalies. There is only one exception: For scenario 6 the linear model is\\r\\nable to detect the sensor fault – recall that in this particular scenario the sen-\\r\\nsor fault is characterized by a slowly overflowing sensor measurements (i.e. the\\r\\npressure value goes to infinity), which is expected to be easily detected because\\r\\nit is a very “loud” fault. The kNN model shows good performance in detecting\\r\\nthe anomaly but has a huge number of false positives – i.e. it is too sensitive and\\r\\nraises lots of false alarms. Our proposed SAM-kNN shows the best performance\\r\\n– it is able to consistently detect the anomalies while having a small number\\r\\nof false positives only. The economic score takes into account both the true positive detections and their distances from the actual leak location. The higher the economic score, the better the model performs. In order to set the threshold for separating leaky from non-leaky edges on the validation split, we used our own implementation of the Economic Score metric2 based on the KIOS research metric of the challenge. This KIOS Economic Score metric adopts a purely economic perspective. In this perspective, the water utility assesses its gains based on the money saved from successfully detecting water leaks. Additionally, the utility takes into account the expenses associated with dispatching a repair crew each time they need to search for a leak. A valid detection is one that identifies a link ID situated within a predefined distance from the actual leak site, and the reported start time of the leakage incident falls within the duration of the same leak. The predefined distance corresponds to the operational capability of the close-range equipment employed by the repair crew [6]. Discussion The BattleDIM Challenge 2020 introduced a metric [6] to evaluate leakage detection algorithms in water supply networks. This metric considers spatial and temporal characteristics of predicted pipe leakages, aiming to capture their economic impact. However in our experiments, optimizing for this metric seems to introduce unintended and non-intuitive artefacts as for example a\\r\\np.9: stringent penalties for undetected or late-identified leakages may inadvertently increase false predictions without proportional consequences. This can impose additional costs on water suppliers. Moreover, the metric only evaluates the initial prediction and does not account for potential improvements over subsequent time steps. This is further distorted by the hierarchical order of evaluation, in which the first detected time-step is taken more into account than the ones that follow in chronological order. The optimization of the model towards a high economic score comes along with an increasing number of false values, which are not ranked high for the\\r\\np.10: penalty calculation. When we use different metrics like F1, Precision, Recall, Balanced Accuracy or AUC-ROC to set the threshold on the validation split the model is optimized towards a higher True Positive rate and focuses more on eliminating false predictions. The highest True Positive Rate we gained was 82,61 percent. If we manually tune the threshold on the validation set, we have been able to gain an Economic Score of 326,521 as highest value but at the cost of 138 False Positives. This means, that if the model is optimized to predict as much as possible as true value the Economic Score rises, but the False values also go up. Nevertheless, the metric’s integration of spatial and temporal failures is commendable, catering to the specific needs of water distribution networks. text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: D:/GitHub/ML NJIT/web/src/miniproject/milestone-2/previous-learning-solutions.md\n+# Previous learning solutions · detailed review\n+\n+**Research companion to the five-page Milestone 2 draft.** This chapter explains the reviewed solutions in greater detail than the submission-length report. The underlying corpus contains 46 PDF files and 11 selected repository snapshots, including versions of the same work and auxiliary sources. The results below belong to their authors; our code inspection was static and their training was not reproduced.\n+\n+## 1. What is being compared\n+\n+Our target is the **recorded pressure of a monitored sensor at t+15 minutes**, using information available when issuing the forecast at t. A work is a direct temporal antecedent only when historical inputs precede its pressure target. A lookback window, alarm persistence, simulation step or Kalman prediction stage does not by itself establish a future-pressure forecast horizon.\n+\n+| Task | Target | Why the distinction matters |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| Spatial reconstruction | Pressure at unobserved nodes at the current time | Uses current sensors and simulated full-state labels; does not anticipate a future measured reading |\n+| Temporal prediction | Pressure after the last input measurement | Direct overlap, but horizon, data and forecast purpose must be checked |\n+| Healthy reference | Pressure expected without a new leak | Useful for diagnostic residuals; may deliberately fail to follow pressure during a leak |\n+| Leak classification/localization | Alarm, pipe, node or zone | Event counts, distance and economic score are not pressure MAE/RMSE |\n+| Sequence reconstruction | The same input sequence reconstructed | An LSTM autoencoder can use temporal context without forecasting a future target |\n+\n+SCADA from the official 2018/2019 release, nominal-model training and new L-Town simulation scenarios are reported separately. A shared topology does not make their evaluation sets interchangeable. Neither 2018 nor 2019 should be assumed globally leak-free.\n+\n+## 2. Historical competition solutions already constrain novelty\n+\n+The 2020 proposals cover data-driven references, hydraulic inverse models, graph interpolation, demand calibration and optimization of leak location. Leakbusters is the predecessor of LILA; Under Pressure uses a pressure–leak dual formulation; Tongji combines calibration, STL and statistical monitoring; IRI combines graph interpolation in area A with hydraulic models in other areas; UNIFE estimates demand patterns and searches candidate leak locations.\n+\n+Two entries are particularly relevant to a forecasting proposal. **Tsinghua uses a multivariate VAR representation of normal pressure patterns before statistical process control. Artesia uses ExtraTrees for mean nighttime pressure**, with daily aggregates and blocks of days for validation. The reviewed proposals do not establish a comparable measured-pressure +15-minute benchmark. Daily summaries also require an availability check before being used as prospective inputs. These precedents rule out presenting “use history,” “multivariate regression” or “use trees” as standalone novelties.\n+\n+The official evaluation rewards water saved and penalizes inspection/localization cost. Its TPR and economic ranking answer the leak-detection challenge, rather than identify a universally best pressure forecaster. The benchmark paper also identifies omitted operational difficulties, including communication delay/loss and substantial measurement-quality problems. That motivates an availability experiment, but does not establish its novelty.\n+\n+Source: [official benchmark paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601), especially PDF pp.11 and 18. All 18 proposals remain represented in the [source matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz).\n+\n+## 3. Garðarsson, Boem and Toni · graph prediction and reconstruction\n+\n+**Task and data.** Two Chebyshev graph networks are trained using pressure generated by the nominal hydraulic model. A reconstructor estimates the current full graph state from sparse pressure sensors; a predictor uses historical sparse pressure to estimate the next state. Their outputs are compared to diagnose leaks in the original SCADA series. The model uses pressure and topology; the article emphasizes that competing entries also use other measurements.\n+\n+**Preparation and model.** The nominal graph and pressure scaling define the training representation. The article uses four Chebyshev convolutional layers, hidden polynomial orders [240,120,20], hidden filter sizes [120,60,30], SiLU activations, sigmoid output, Xavier initialization and Adam with weight decay. Histories of 1,2,3,6,12 and 24 samples are considered; T=3 is selected. **That is a 15-minute history, not a 15-minute forecast.**\n+\n+**Temporal alignment.** In the selected `data_loader.py`, a window ends at row i−1 and its prediction label is row i. At five-minute sampling this establishes a **one-step/+5-minute horizon**. Current reconstruction instead takes its label from row i−1. This code distinction resolves the ambiguity created by treating every learned pressure estimate as a forecast.\n+\n+**Diagnostic evaluation.** Node residuals are predictor minus reconstructor; neighboring node differences create edge residuals. The article uses seven-day moving statistics, a threshold parameter alpha=1, mean correction using identified non-faulty periods and filtering to suppress repeated alarms within a six-hop neighborhood. Its configuration reports 19 TP, 138 FP, four FN and an economic score of EUR326,521. Those are diagnostic/event results, not pressure MAE at +15 minutes. The false-alarm burden remains material.\n+\n+**Code and reproducibility.** The inspected predictor splits nominal data 80/20 with `shuffle=False`, while its scaler is computed before that split. Some application calls omit temporal-mode arguments and inherit reconstruction defaults, creating a static channel/recipe concern. We did not execute the code and do not conclude that every notebook fails. A causal reproduction must explicitly fix modes, scaling, targets and data boundaries.\n+\n+**Relation to our proposal.** This is a direct pressure-prediction antecedent. Extending +5 to +15 minutes and evaluating recorded pressure against persistence are useful changes, but the mere existence of a temporal or topology-aware predictor is already established. A healthy-reference model optimized for leakage diagnosis has a different purpose from following observed pressure during a continuing leak.\n+\n+[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ifacol.2022.07.203), PDF pp.3–5; [selected repository](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/tree/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8), commit `864a99041af6`, particularly `data_loader.py` and `predict.py`.\n+\n+## 4. Jakob et al. · SAM-kNN adaptive virtual sensors\n+\n+**Task and data.** The paper constructs ten three-month WNTR scenarios for modified L-Town area A: 661 nodes, 766 links and 29 pressure sensors, sampled every five minutes. Five scenarios contain one leak each; five contain a sensor fault, including drift/overflow, noise, offset, zero readings or a shift. These are additional scenarios, not the complete original 2019 mixture of simultaneous leaks.\n+\n+**Model.** For each pressure sensor, a virtual sensor predicts from the other pressure sensors. SAM-kNN combines short-term, long-term and combined memories, tracks their errors, chooses a memory and cleans/compresses examples to adapt to drift while retaining previous concepts. Predicted-versus-observed discrepancies trigger anomaly alarms. It is more than ordinary fixed-window kNN.\n+\n+**Verified time construction.** The paper describes a four-sample window; the exact selected preprocessing is more precise: with `time_win_len=4`, X averages rows t,t+1,t+2 and Y takes row t+3. Relative to the last input, that is **the next five-minute sample**. The regressor excludes the target sensor's input when creating its virtual sensor. It must not be characterized as merely spatial reconstruction.\n+\n+**Evaluation and results.** The scenarios are streamed in batches of 200. The selected experiment predicts/detects a batch before updating with that batch's labels. The leakage table reports TP=1 and FN=0 for each of the five scenarios, with FP=[48,20,3,20,17]. Crucially, TP/FN indicate whether the single event was detected, whereas every individual false alarm contributes to FP. Those counts cannot be pooled as if they were balanced row-wise classification statistics, and are not pressure forecast MAE.\n+\n+**Limits and relation.** The repository states that its scenario data are not distributed; training was not reproduced here. Adaptation already has a direct antecedent. For an operational +15-minute extension, an update using the label of origin u is permitted only after that target is observed at u+15. Batch order alone does not prove equivalent forecast availability or real-time detection delay.\n+\n+[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62), PDF pp.8–10; [preprocessing](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py); [experiments](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/experiments.py), commit `9116ec99bf9f`.\n+\n+## 5. Daniel et al. · LILA and a healthy pressure reference\n+\n+**Task and preparation.** LILA identifies leaks from pairwise pressure relations and flow-related terms, then localizes candidate pipes with a calibrated hydraulic model. Reference periods and suitable sensor combinations are selected to separate overlapping leaks. Current pressure is estimated from measurements belonging to the same hydraulic condition; the target is not pressure at t+15.\n+\n+**Model and contribution.** Linear regression generates relative-error trajectories; CUSUM identifies changes and a most-affected sensor. The localization stage uses pressure-drop signatures and optimization, retaining up to 30 candidate pipes and a limited set of sensors near the most-affected sensor. The expanded work adds automated change-point detection and improves the localization recipe over the 2020 Leakbusters submission. Historical contest performance and expanded-paper performance are distinct versions.\n+\n+**Evaluation must be read literally.** The expanded paper evaluates the 19 leaks starting in 2019 and excludes four leaks carried over from 2018. At a 300 m location tolerance and detection allowed during the leak lifespan, Table 1 reports TP=17, FP=2, FN=0, recall=1.000, precision=0.895, F1=0.944 and EUR307,852. All 19 leaks were identified; two localizations outside the permitted radius were counted as FP. Consequently FN=0 and recall=1 do not mean that all 19 passed the location criterion.\n+\n+**Timing and foresight.** The claim of locating every event within 374 m requires the paper's wider sensitivity setting: a maximum allowed detection delay of 41 days. It is not a rapid-detection guarantee. Most abrupt leaks are detected much sooner, but p280 takes approximately 116 hours; incipient events have around 20-day mean CUSUM delay in the reported analysis. Expert annotations were made ex post, and the paper acknowledges their perfect-foresight advantage. A blind prospective procedure must not inherit those annotations as operational inputs.\n+\n+**Code reading.** The selected `leak_analysis` implementation includes current reference pressure and raw `PUMP_1` flow in LinearRegression; the published formulation includes squared flow terms. The exact feature recipe therefore needs version-specific checking. Perfect-team trajectories and event-specific reference windows serve retrospective analysis; they are not legitimate future-pressure predictors merely because the truth is now public.\n+\n+**Relation to our target.** LILA is an accessible regression/CUSUM precedent and a strong illustration of why healthy-reference estimation differs from observed-pressure forecasting. Sensor selection, overlapping-leak handling and hydraulic context already exist; our contribution cannot claim to introduce those ideas in general.\n+\n+[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535), PDF pp.4 and 7–9; [selected implementation](https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA/blob/b488a60db1974b6f5b7887ce1cf5d31dff3dad68/_utils/BattLeDIM_utils.py); [localization repository](https://github.com/jorgeps86/LeakLocalization). LILA snapshot commit `b488a60db197`.\n+\n+## 6. Zhang and Fink · AIGNN\n+\n+**Task and architecture.** A neural algorithmic reasoner is pretrained on Ford–Fulkerson max-flow trajectories from 1,000 random 16-node graphs. Its processor is transferred into pressure reconstruction/prediction with new encoders/decoders. ChebNetIN and ChebNetEMB inject learned embeddings as additional inputs or internal features. Algorithm-informed embeddings do not by themselves constitute a proof of exact hydraulic conservation.\n+\n+**Data and alignment.** The nominal hydraulic model generates pressure-training labels. A reconstructor uses sparse pressure at t; a predictor uses t−T through t−1 to estimate t. The next-step formulation is therefore another direct temporal antecedent. SCADA 2018 determines diagnostic thresholds and 2019 evaluates detection/localization; sensor-relocation experiments assess generalization.\n+\n+**Results and version control.** In the local 2024 preprint, Table 3 reports overall relative prediction errors of 0.3774±0.1588% for ChebNet, 0.3249±0.0685% for AIGNN, 0.2964±0.0874% for ChebNetIN and 0.2077±0.0074% for ChebNetEMB. These are the paper's norm-based relative errors, not MAE in metres or a persistence comparison at +15 minutes. Reconstruction Table 2, prediction Table 3 and relocation Table 4 must not be mixed. The published 2026 version has additional false-alarm reporting; its figures are not silently merged with the preprint.\n+\n+**Relation.** The work establishes temporal graph prediction, max-flow-informed transfer and sensitivity to sensor placement. It prevents presenting topology or algorithmic features alone as our novelty. No selected author-code snapshot for AIGNN is claimed in our repository inventory.\n+\n+[Local preprint source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.02797), PDF pp.15–21; [published version](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ress.2025.111494).\n+\n+## 7. Schaller et al. · Liquor-HGNN\n+\n+**Task/model.** A heterogeneous graph distinguishes monitored/unmonitored nodes and four relation types. NeuralProphet fills demand information; the graph classifier assigns leak probabilities to links using binary cross-entropy. The selected implementation labels the same snapshot as its pressure inputs. It does not regress pressure into the future.\n+\n+**Protocol/results.** The paper uses 80% of 2018 for training, 20% for validation/threshold selection and 2019 for test. Its Table 1 reports TPR=47.83%, 46 FP and EUR271,584. The conserved 2026 README instead reports 11 TP, 51 FP, 12 FN and EUR282,785.1. These versions remain separate. The discussion explains why optimizing economic score may increase false alarms; no ranking is transferred to our forecasting task.\n+\n+**Static concerns.** The selected snapshot computes mode-specific scales, including test data. Its `expected_demands.py` reads a 2018 demand filename for the variable named 2019 and fits annual demand models in ways that require an availability audit. These are concerns in that code recipe, not a reproduced demonstration that the 2023 paper's experiment is wrong. Simulated demands/flows are additional resources, not automatically available observed measurements.\n+\n+[Paper](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-3630/LWDA2023-paper40.pdf), PDF pp.7–10; [snapshot](https://github.com/MilanShao/Liquor-HGNN/tree/64ff247902e702d89400eac74f06aac45d2ea292), commit `64ff247902e7`.\n+\n+## 8. Truong et al. · GATRes\n+\n+**Task/protocol.** Residual graph attention reconstructs the current pressure state from masked current observations. Training snapshots are simulated, with sparse-sensor masks; robustness to sensor positions, mask rates, noise and shifted demand distributions is studied. The benchmark comparison uses generated snapshots split 60/20/20 and a separate demand-pattern evaluation. L-Town's latter test is a one-week simulation at five-minute resolution, not the official measured-pressure forecast in 2019.\n+\n+**Results depend on the test.** Table 10 reports L-Town MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m for GATRes, 1.5928±0.0050 m for mGCN and 3.4505±0.0129 m for GraphConvWat. On same-generation-distribution snapshots in Table 11, mGCN has MAE=0.2441±0.0014 m versus GATRes=0.4930±0.0028 m. A model ranking therefore cannot be quoted without its protocol. A five-minute sampling interval does not turn this spatial reconstruction into a five-minute forecast.\n+\n+**Code/relevance.** The selected training masks `data.x` while comparing with the same snapshot's `data.y`; train statistics are passed to validation. This is a useful preparation reference. Sparse sensors and robustness are established topics; our extension needs a specific prospective forecast and reading-age question.\n+\n+[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1029/2023WR036741), PDF pp.19–21; [snapshot](https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation/tree/bb287ed2ae091b29fa19f2417406756b57a45a1f), commit `bb287ed2ae09`.\n+\n+## 9. Other verified families and their scope\n+\n+| Work | Data and learning target | Contribution/result | Boundary for our proposal |\n+|---|---|---|---|\n+| [GSI + dictionary learning](https://doi.org/10.1109/CCTA49430.2022.9966160) | New area-A scenarios: 30 leak nodes, four-day train/three-day test | Virtual head interpolation and multiple dictionaries/voting; reported accuracy up to 96.54% | Leak-location classification, not original 2019 pressure forecasting |\n+| [Rajabi et al., GAN + SSIM](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2023.120012) | Generated nominal demand/pressure maps and selected leak cases | Conditional GAN, kriging and SSIM; map MAPE 1.42% with 780 points and 0.67% with 33 | Current-condition healthy maps; five consecutive exceedances are alarm persistence, not forecast horizon |\n+| [Doss et al., AE/VAE/LSTM-AE](https://doi.org/10.2166/ws.2024.174) | Nominal training and 33 separate simulated single-leak scenarios | Reconstructs 144-sample/12-hour sequences and detects changes | Same-sequence reconstruction; recalibration already exists |\n+\n+SICAMS also illustrates deterioration in water-loss regression during the second half of 2019, supporting the need to test regime change. Its sensor-fault examples use synthetic perturbations, not recorded original fault labels. The selected [SIAM code](https://github.com/dorofeev2025/SIAM/tree/7e8970700212229ea6e715274790e6dc9536c03d) is an area-A 2018 demonstration, not the entire paper pipeline. PELT classification and post-event analysis must be distinguished from prospective alarm issuance.\n+\n+## 10. Pending and auxiliary sources\n+\n+**AGN (2025)** remains partially verified: its title describes demand-state forecasting, while the consulted formulation/metrics involve hydraulic states and metres. The exact target, horizon and code remain unresolved. It uses a modified L-Town scenario with a chronological 70/20/10 split. It is neither discarded as unrelated nor cited as proof of an identical +15-minute benchmark. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911).\n+\n+**GASTL (2025)** concerns cross-network pressure estimation/transfer under limited sensors. The editorial consultation does not substitute a validated local full PDF or an original-SCADA forecast protocol. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2025.124475).\n+\n+hDA-RNN/hDS-RNN forecasts pressure/flow on another real network, sampled every 30 minutes; its four-step horizon is two hours. WeLL uses other networks, and the centrality-aware study also concerns other networks. They establish external methodological precedents but are not L-Town results. DiTEC generated datasets and EPyT-Flow are infrastructure; GraphConvWat is a reconstruction antecedent. Availability of their repositories is not proof of a reproduced result on our task.\n+\n+## 11. What can be claimed after this comparison\n+\n+Pressure prediction, temporal history, topology, sparse sensors, adaptation, ensembles and robustness all have precedents. The reviewed corpus has not established a homogeneous solution/evaluation for our exact **observed-pressure +15-minute official-SCADA task, equal information budgets, strong causal baselines and separately controlled reading age**. This is a gap in the reviewed corpus, not proof of worldwide novelty.\n+\n+Our contribution still needs a specified rule and experimental evidence. Every comparison must align target, horizon, sensor coverage, data origin, units, chronological boundaries, tuning budget and availability. Leak truth may annotate evaluation regimes, but must not select test-based hyperparameters or enter operational forecast inputs. These conclusions preserve the Milestone 1 objective while recognizing what the literature already contributes.\n+\n+## 12. Traceability and limitations\n+\n+The [full state of the art](#/miniproject/milestone-2/estado-del-arte), [46-file matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz), [repository index](#/miniproject/milestone-2/repositorios) and [static code review](#/miniproject/milestone-2/codigo-y-curso) contain the supporting versions, page references and code evidence. This chapter prioritizes task-relevant detail; it does not turn every snapshot into a complete executable clone or every result into a verified reproduction.\n+\n+The report uses a shorter synthesis across its two literature pages. This companion remains the detailed research repository, so the five-page limit does not force the evidence and qualifications to disappear.\n*** End Patch"));
```
