    "D:\\GitHub\\ML NJIT\\web\\src\\miniproject\\milestone-2\\previous-learning-solutions.md": {
      "type": "add",
      "content": "# Previous learning solutions · detailed review\n\n**Research companion to the five-page Milestone 2 draft.** This chapter explains the reviewed solutions in greater detail than the submission-length report. The underlying corpus contains 46 PDF files and 11 selected repository snapshots, including versions of the same work and auxiliary sources. The results below belong to their authors; our code inspection was static and their training was not reproduced.\n\n## 1. What is being compared\n\nOur target is the **recorded pressure of a monitored sensor at t+15 minutes**, using information available when issuing the forecast at t. A work is a direct temporal antecedent only when historical inputs precede its pressure target. A lookback window, alarm persistence, simulation step or Kalman prediction stage does not by itself establish a future-pressure forecast horizon.\n\n| Task | Target | Why the distinction matters |\n|---|---|---|\n| Spatial reconstruction | Pressure at unobserved nodes at the current time | Uses current sensors and simulated full-state labels; does not anticipate a future measured reading |\n| Temporal prediction | Pressure after the last input measurement | Direct overlap, but horizon, data and forecast purpose must be checked |\n| Healthy reference | Pressure expected without a new leak | Useful for diagnostic residuals; may deliberately fail to follow pressure during a leak |\n| Leak classification/localization | Alarm, pipe, node or zone | Event counts, distance and economic score are not pressure MAE/RMSE |\n| Sequence reconstruction | The same input sequence reconstructed | An LSTM autoencoder can use temporal context without forecasting a future target |\n\nSCADA from the official 2018/2019 release, nominal-model training and new L-Town simulation scenarios are reported separately. A shared topology does not make their evaluation sets interchangeable. Neither 2018 nor 2019 should be assumed globally leak-free.\n\n## 2. Historical competition solutions already constrain novelty\n\nThe 2020 proposals cover data-driven references, hydraulic inverse models, graph interpolation, demand calibration and optimization of leak location. Leakbusters is the predecessor of LILA; Under Pressure uses a pressure–leak dual formulation; Tongji combines calibration, STL and statistical monitoring; IRI combines graph interpolation in area A with hydraulic models in other areas; UNIFE estimates demand patterns and searches candidate leak locations.\n\nTwo entries are particularly relevant to a forecasting proposal. **Tsinghua uses a multivariate VAR representation of normal pressure patterns before statistical process control. Artesia uses ExtraTrees for mean nighttime pressure**, with daily aggregates and blocks of days for validation. The reviewed proposals do not establish a comparable measured-pressure +15-minute benchmark. Daily summaries also require an availability check before being used as prospective inputs. These precedents rule out presenting “use history,” “multivariate regression” or “use trees” as standalone novelties.\n\nThe official evaluation rewards water saved and penalizes inspection/localization cost. Its TPR and economic ranking answer the leak-detection challenge, rather than identify a universally best pressure forecaster. The benchmark paper also identifies omitted operational difficulties, including communication delay/loss and substantial measurement-quality problems. That motivates an availability experiment, but does not establish its novelty.\n\nSource: [official benchmark paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601), especially PDF pp.11 and 18. All 18 proposals remain represented in the [source matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz).\n\n## 3. Garðarsson, Boem and Toni · graph prediction and reconstruction\n\n**Task and data.** Two Chebyshev graph networks are trained using pressure generated by the nominal hydraulic model. A reconstructor estimates the current full graph state from sparse pressure sensors; a predictor uses historical sparse pressure to estimate the next state. Their outputs are compared to diagnose leaks in the original SCADA series. The model uses pressure and topology; the article emphasizes that competing entries also use other measurements.\n\n**Preparation and model.** The nominal graph and pressure scaling define the training representation. The article uses four Chebyshev convolutional layers, hidden polynomial orders [240,120,20], hidden filter sizes [120,60,30], SiLU activations, sigmoid output, Xavier initialization and Adam with weight decay. Histories of 1,2,3,6,12 and 24 samples are considered; T=3 is selected. **That is a 15-minute history, not a 15-minute forecast.**\n\n**Temporal alignment.** In the selected `data_loader.py`, a window ends at row i−1 and its prediction label is row i. At five-minute sampling this establishes a **one-step/+5-minute horizon**. Current reconstruction instead takes its label from row i−1. This code distinction resolves the ambiguity created by treating every learned pressure estimate as a forecast.\n\n**Diagnostic evaluation.** Node residuals are predictor minus reconstructor; neighboring node differences create edge residuals. The article uses seven-day moving statistics, a threshold parameter alpha=1, mean correction using identified non-faulty periods and filtering to suppress repeated alarms within a six-hop neighborhood. Its configuration reports 19 TP, 138 FP, four FN and an economic score of EUR326,521. Those are diagnostic/event results, not pressure MAE at +15 minutes. The false-alarm burden remains material.\n\n**Code and reproducibility.** The inspected predictor splits nominal data 80/20 with `shuffle=False`, while its scaler is computed before that split. Some application calls omit temporal-mode arguments and inherit reconstruction defaults, creating a static channel/recipe concern. We did not execute the code and do not conclude that every notebook fails. A causal reproduction must explicitly fix modes, scaling, targets and data boundaries.\n\n**Relation to our proposal.** This is a direct pressure-prediction antecedent. Extending +5 to +15 minutes and evaluating recorded pressure against persistence are useful changes, but the mere existence of a temporal or topology-aware predictor is already established. A healthy-reference model optimized for leakage diagnosis has a different purpose from following observed pressure during a continuing leak.\n\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ifacol.2022.07.203), PDF pp.3–5; [selected repository](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/tree/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8), commit `864a99041af6`, particularly `data_loader.py` and `predict.py`.\n\n## 4. Jakob et al. · SAM-kNN adaptive virtual sensors\n\n**Task and data.** The paper constructs ten three-month WNTR scenarios for modified L-Town area A: 661 nodes, 766 links and 29 pressure sensors, sampled every five minutes. Five scenarios contain one leak each; five contain a sensor fault, including drift/overflow, noise, offset, zero readings or a shift. These are additional scenarios, not the complete original 2019 mixture of simultaneous leaks.\n\n**Model.** For each pressure sensor, a virtual sensor predicts from the other pressure sensors. SAM-kNN combines short-term, long-term and combined memories, tracks their errors, chooses a memory and cleans/compresses examples to adapt to drift while retaining previous concepts. Predicted-versus-observed discrepancies trigger anomaly alarms. It is more than ordinary fixed-window kNN.\n\n**Verified time construction.** The paper describes a four-sample window; the exact selected preprocessing is more precise: with `time_win_len=4`, X averages rows t,t+1,t+2 and Y takes row t+3. Relative to the last input, that is **the next five-minute sample**. The regressor excludes the target sensor's input when creating its virtual sensor. It must not be characterized as merely spatial reconstruction.\n\n**Evaluation and results.** The scenarios are streamed in batches of 200. The selected experiment predicts/detects a batch before updating with that batch's labels. The leakage table reports TP=1 and FN=0 for each of the five scenarios, with FP=[48,20,3,20,17]. Crucially, TP/FN indicate whether the single event was detected, whereas every individual false alarm contributes to FP. Those counts cannot be pooled as if they were balanced row-wise classification statistics, and are not pressure forecast MAE.\n\n**Limits and relation.** The repository states that its scenario data are not distributed; training was not reproduced here. Adaptation already has a direct antecedent. For an operational +15-minute extension, an update using the label of origin u is permitted only after that target is observed at u+15. Batch order alone does not prove equivalent forecast availability or real-time detection delay.\n\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62), PDF pp.8–10; [preprocessing](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py); [experiments](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/experiments.py), commit `9116ec99bf9f`.\n\n## 5. Daniel et al. · LILA and a healthy pressure reference\n\n**Task and preparation.** LILA identifies leaks from pairwise pressure relations and flow-related terms, then localizes candidate pipes with a calibrated hydraulic model. Reference periods and suitable sensor combinations are selected to separate overlapping leaks. Current pressure is estimated from measurements belonging to the same hydraulic condition; the target is not pressure at t+15.\n\n**Model and contribution.** Linear regression generates relative-error trajectories; CUSUM identifies changes and a most-affected sensor. The localization stage uses pressure-drop signatures and optimization, retaining up to 30 candidate pipes and a limited set of sensors near the most-affected sensor. The expanded work adds automated change-point detection and improves the localization recipe over the 2020 Leakbusters submission. Historical contest performance and expanded-paper performance are distinct versions.\n\n**Evaluation must be read literally.** The expanded paper evaluates the 19 leaks starting in 2019 and excludes four leaks carried over from 2018. At a 300 m location tolerance and detection allowed during the leak lifespan, Table 1 reports TP=17, FP=2, FN=0, recall=1.000, precision=0.895, F1=0.944 and EUR307,852. All 19 leaks were identified; two localizations outside the permitted radius were counted as FP. Consequently FN=0 and recall=1 do not mean that all 19 passed the location criterion.\n\n**Timing and foresight.** The claim of locating every event within 374 m requires the paper's wider sensitivity setting: a maximum allowed detection delay of 41 days. It is not a rapid-detection guarantee. Most abrupt leaks are detected much sooner, but p280 takes approximately 116 hours; incipient events have around 20-day mean CUSUM delay in the reported analysis. Expert annotations were made ex post, and the paper acknowledges their perfect-foresight advantage. A blind prospective procedure must not inherit those annotations as operational inputs.\n\n**Code reading.** The selected `leak_analysis` implementation includes current reference pressure and raw `PUMP_1` flow in LinearRegression; the published formulation includes squared flow terms. The exact feature recipe therefore needs version-specific checking. Perfect-team trajectories and event-specific reference windows serve retrospective analysis; they are not legitimate future-pressure predictors merely because the truth is now public.\n\n**Relation to our target.** LILA is an accessible regression/CUSUM precedent and a strong illustration of why healthy-reference estimation differs from observed-pressure forecasting. Sensor selection, overlapping-leak handling and hydraulic context already exist; our contribution cannot claim to introduce those ideas in general.\n\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535), PDF pp.4 and 7–9; [selected implementation](https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA/blob/b488a60db1974b6f5b7887ce1cf5d31dff3dad68/_utils/BattLeDIM_utils.py); [localization repository](https://github.com/jorgeps86/LeakLocalization). LILA snapshot commit `b488a60db197`.\n\n## 6. Zhang and Fink · AIGNN\n\n**Task and architecture.** A neural algorithmic reasoner is pretrained on Ford–Fulkerson max-flow trajectories from 1,000 random 16-node graphs. Its processor is transferred into pressure reconstruction/prediction with new encoders/decoders. ChebNetIN and ChebNetEMB inject learned embeddings as additional inputs or internal features. Algorithm-informed embeddings do not by themselves constitute a proof of exact hydraulic conservation.\n\n**Data and alignment.** The nominal hydraulic model generates pressure-training labels. A reconstructor uses sparse pressure at t; a predictor uses t−T through t−1 to estimate t. The next-step formulation is therefore another direct temporal antecedent. SCADA 2018 determines diagnostic thresholds and 2019 evaluates detection/localization; sensor-relocation experiments assess generalization.\n\n**Results and version control.** In the local 2024 preprint, Table 3 reports overall relative prediction errors of 0.3774±0.1588% for ChebNet, 0.3249±0.0685% for AIGNN, 0.2964±0.0874% for ChebNetIN and 0.2077±0.0074% for ChebNetEMB. These are the paper's norm-based relative errors, not MAE in metres or a persistence comparison at +15 minutes. Reconstruction Table 2, prediction Table 3 and relocation Table 4 must not be mixed. The published 2026 version has additional false-alarm reporting; its figures are not silently merged with the preprint.\n\n**Relation.** The work establishes temporal graph prediction, max-flow-informed transfer and sensitivity to sensor placement. It prevents presenting topology or algorithmic features alone as our novelty. No selected author-code snapshot for AIGNN is claimed in our repository inventory.\n\n[Local preprint source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.02797), PDF pp.15–21; [published version](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ress.2025.111494).\n\n## 7. Schaller et al. · Liquor-HGNN\n\n**Task/model.** A heterogeneous graph distinguishes monitored/unmonitored nodes and four relation types. NeuralProphet fills demand information; the graph classifier assigns leak probabilities to links using binary cross-entropy. The selected implementation labels the same snapshot as its pressure inputs. It does not regress pressure into the future.\n\n**Protocol/results.** The paper uses 80% of 2018 for training, 20% for validation/threshold selection and 2019 for test. Its Table 1 reports TPR=47.83%, 46 FP and EUR271,584. The conserved 2026 README instead reports 11 TP, 51 FP, 12 FN and EUR282,785.1. These versions remain separate. The discussion explains why optimizing economic score may increase false alarms; no ranking is transferred to our forecasting task.\n\n**Static concerns.** The selected snapshot computes mode-specific scales, including test data. Its `expected_demands.py` reads a 2018 demand filename for the variable named 2019 and fits annual demand models in ways that require an availability audit. These are concerns in that code recipe, not a reproduced demonstration that the 2023 paper's experiment is wrong. Simulated demands/flows are additional resources, not automatically available observed measurements.\n\n[Paper](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-3630/LWDA2023-paper40.pdf), PDF pp.7–10; [snapshot](https://github.com/MilanShao/Liquor-HGNN/tree/64ff247902e702d89400eac74f06aac45d2ea292), commit `64ff247902e7`.\n\n## 8. Truong et al. · GATRes\n\n**Task/protocol.** Residual graph attention reconstructs the current pressure state from masked current observations. Training snapshots are simulated, with sparse-sensor masks; robustness to sensor positions, mask rates, noise and shifted demand distributions is studied. The benchmark comparison uses generated snapshots split 60/20/20 and a separate demand-pattern evaluation. L-Town's latter test is a one-week simulation at five-minute resolution, not the official measured-pressure forecast in 2019.\n\n**Results depend on the test.** Table 10 reports L-Town MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m for GATRes, 1.5928±0.0050 m for mGCN and 3.4505±0.0129 m for GraphConvWat. On same-generation-distribution snapshots in Table 11, mGCN has MAE=0.2441±0.0014 m versus GATRes=0.4930±0.0028 m. A model ranking therefore cannot be quoted without its protocol. A five-minute sampling interval does not turn this spatial reconstruction into a five-minute forecast.\n\n**Code/relevance.** The selected training masks `data.x` while comparing with the same snapshot's `data.y`; train statistics are passed to validation. This is a useful preparation reference. Sparse sensors and robustness are established topics; our extension needs a specific prospective forecast and reading-age question.\n\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1029/2023WR036741), PDF pp.19–21; [snapshot](https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation/tree/bb287ed2ae091b29fa19f2417406756b57a45a1f), commit `bb287ed2ae09`.\n\n## 9. Other verified families and their scope\n\n| Work | Data and learning target | Contribution/result | Boundary for our proposal |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| [GSI + dictionary learning](https://doi.org/10.1109/CCTA49430.2022.9966160) | New area-A scenarios: 30 leak nodes, four-day train/three-day test | Virtual head interpolation and multiple dictionaries/voting; reported accuracy up to 96.54% | Leak-location classification, not original 2019 pressure forecasting |\n| [Rajabi et al., GAN + SSIM](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2023.120012) | Generated nominal demand/pressure maps and selected leak cases | Conditional GAN, kriging and SSIM; map MAPE 1.42% with 780 points and 0.67% with 33 | Current-condition healthy maps; five consecutive exceedances are alarm persistence, not forecast horizon |\n| [Doss et al., AE/VAE/LSTM-AE](https://doi.org/10.2166/ws.2024.174) | Nominal training and 33 separate simulated single-leak scenarios | Reconstructs 144-sample/12-hour sequences and detects changes | Same-sequence reconstruction; different simultaneous-leak mixture from official SCADA |\n| [Mobile sensor method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.19900) | Own scenarios plus a p257 case | Iterative placement improves localization with few fixed/mobile sensors | Sensor budget is already studied; relocation adds operational delay |\n| [D-UKF-AW-GSI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.11687) | Current-state fusion and localization; some tests add 100 simulated AMR sensors in A | Graph interpolation plus Kalman head/flow estimates | Kalman prediction is not a validated observed-pressure +15-minute benchmark |\n| [Topological signal processing](https://www.eurasip.org/Proceedings/Eusipco/Eusipco2025/pdfs/0002407.pdf) | Synthetic flow signals on L-Town topology | Cell-complex diffusion and sparse anomaly inference | Does not use the original measurement coverage as our test |\n| [Li and Chen, ensemble](https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220) | 1,050 generated nighttime scenarios; 840/210 stratified split | Seven-zone RF/XGBoost/MLP stacking, eight selected sensors, SHAP; accuracy/macro-F1≈0.9667/0.9666 | Zonal classification; topology, feature selection, ensembles and interpretability already combined |\n| [SICAMS](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eswa.2026.131450) | Official SCADA plus additional simulations | Whitening/Mahalanobis, Hotelling T², hysteresis, PELT, sensor attribution and Laplacian prelocalization | Detection/classification/current loss estimate; recalibration already exists |\n\nSICAMS also illustrates deterioration in water-loss regression during the second half of 2019, supporting the need to test regime change. Its sensor-fault examples use synthetic perturbations, not recorded original fault labels. The selected [SIAM code](https://github.com/dorofeev2025/SIAM/tree/7e8970700212229ea6e715274790e6dc9536c03d) is an area-A 2018 demonstration, not the entire paper pipeline. PELT classification and post-event analysis must be distinguished from prospective alarm issuance.\n\n## 10. Pending and auxiliary sources\n\n**AGN (2025)** remains partially verified: its title describes demand-state forecasting, while the consulted formulation/metrics involve hydraulic states and metres. The exact target, horizon and code remain unresolved. It uses a modified L-Town scenario with a chronological 70/20/10 split. It is neither discarded as unrelated nor cited as proof of an identical +15-minute benchmark. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911).\n\n**GASTL (2025)** concerns cross-network pressure estimation/transfer under limited sensors. The editorial consultation does not substitute a validated local full PDF or an original-SCADA forecast protocol. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2025.124475).\n\nhDA-RNN/hDS-RNN forecasts pressure/flow on another real network, sampled every 30 minutes; its four-step horizon is two hours. WeLL uses other networks, and the centrality-aware study also concerns other networks. They establish external methodological precedents but are not L-Town results. DiTEC generated datasets and EPyT-Flow are infrastructure; GraphConvWat is a reconstruction antecedent. Availability of their repositories is not proof of a reproduced result on our task.\n\n## 11. What can be claimed after this comparison\n\nPressure prediction, temporal history, topology, sparse sensors, adaptation, ensembles and robustness all have precedents. The reviewed corpus has not established a homogeneous solution/evaluation for our exact **observed-pressure +15-minute official-SCADA task, equal information budgets, strong causal baselines and separately controlled reading age**. This is a gap in the reviewed corpus, not proof of worldwide novelty.\n\nOur contribution still needs a specified rule and experimental evidence. Every comparison must align target, horizon, sensor coverage, data origin, units, chronological boundaries, tuning budget and availability. Leak truth may annotate evaluation regimes, but must not select test-based hyperparameters or enter operational forecast inputs. These conclusions preserve the Milestone 1 objective while recognizing what the literature already contributes.\n\n## 12. Traceability and limitations\n\nThe [full state of the art](#/miniproject/milestone-2/estado-del-arte), [46-file matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz), [repository index](#/miniproject/milestone-2/repositorios) and [static code review](#/miniproject/milestone-2/codigo-y-curso) contain the supporting versions, page references and code evidence. This chapter prioritizes task-relevant detail; it does not turn every snapshot into a complete executable clone or every result into a verified reproduction.\n\nThe report uses a shorter synthesis across its two literature pages. This companion remains the detailed research repository, so the five-page limit does not force the evidence and qualifications to disappear.\n"
